========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thanks for seeing me. I came in because... I don't know, for a long time now there's just been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me most days. I'm still getting through my driving routes, though. I drive a truck, mostly regional, and let me tell you, the traffic on the interstate lately has been an absolute joke. They've had this one lane closed for construction for what feels like three years, and it just backs everything up for miles. I sit there burning daylight just staring at the bumper in front of me. 

Anyway, I still do that, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts at the animal shelter. We actually just got a litter of beagle mixes in that chew on absolutely everything. One of them got ahold of my good work boots last Tuesday and just went to town on the laces. But yeah, even with all that going on, it just feels like I'm going through the motions. Like I'm not really present in my own life, you know? Just... heavy.

I mean... I don't really know. It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? We had to watch it for a week back in '08 and it chewed up all the wooden baseboards in our front hallway. Cost me a couple hundred bucks from my security deposit when we finally moved out of that rental...

Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. We had his service out at this little cemetery near the county line, and I remember it was raining so hard the mud was up to our ankles. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. 

I tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, "Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball." But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.

I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. You know, speaking of routines, they just changed up my route at work. Used to be I'd take the 95 straight down, hit the same three stops by noon. Now they've got me cutting through these back roads, and let me tell you, the potholes on County Road 9 are something else. I hit one last Tuesday that practically rattled my teeth loose. Spilled half my coffee right onto the dashboard. And it wasn't even the good coffee, it was that cheap stuff from the breakroom that tastes like burnt popcorn. 

I tried telling dispatch about the roads, this guy named Gary. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, "Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road." He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.

We actually just got a litter of beagle mixes in that chew on absolutely everything. One of them got ahold of my good work boots last Tuesday and just went to town on the laces. But yeah, even with all that going on, it just feels like I'm going through the motions. Like I'm not really present in my own life, you know? Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy in the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering at the animal shelter?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if \"joy\" is the right word anymore. I still go, but... it's just something I do. Like, I'll be there cleaning out the kennels or walking them, and it's fine, but I don't get that spark from it like I used to. Speaking of the kennels, they just switched the brand of cleaner they use, and man, it smells like straight industrial bleach mixed with cheap pine needles. It gives me a headache if I'm in the back room for too long. I told the manager, Sarah—she's a nice lady, used to work at the post office before she retired—I told her we need to go back to the old stuff. She said it's a budget thing, which I get, you know? Everything's getting more expensive. Even diesel for my truck is up another ten cents this week. But yeah, the shelter... It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? Place is supposed to have the best meatloaf. I used to really look forward to a good meatloaf, you know, with the ketchup glaze baked on top like my mom used to make. But this place, they put some kind of weird brown gravy all over it. Swear it looked just like 10W-30 motor oil. And the waitress, she was nice enough, but she kept calling me \"hon\" in this real high-pitched voice. Sounded exactly like my ex-wife's sister, Brenda. Man, Brenda had this little toy poodle that just barked at the wind. I swear that dog didn't sleep, ever. We had to watch it for a week back in '08 and it chewed up all the wooden baseboards in our front hallway. I just don't really feel much of anything most of the time. Just flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. There was this place off exit 42, hand-wash only. The guy who ran it, Sal, he had this old pressure washer that sounded like a jet engine taking off. You could hear it from the highway. And wouldn't you know it, it rained the very next morning. Just my luck, right? Rained so hard the wipers couldn't keep up. That's the thing about weather around here, it just turns on a dime. Like last week, they said it was going to be sunny, and I ended up driving through a hail storm near Scranton. Sounded like gravel hitting the roof.

I'm still getting through my driving routes, though. I drive a truck, mostly regional, and let me tell you, the traffic on the interstate lately has been an absolute joke. They've had this one lane closed for construction for what feels like three years, and it just backs everything up for miles. I sit there burning daylight just staring at the bumper in front of me. \n\nAnyway, I still do that, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts at the animal shelter. We actually just got a litter of beagle mixes in that chew on absolutely everything. One of them got ahold of my good work boots last Tuesday and just went to town on the laces. But yeah, even with all that going on, it just feels like I'm going through the motions. Like I'm not really present in my own life, you know? Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy in the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering at the animal shelter?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if \"joy\" is the right word anymore. I still go, but... it's just something I do. Like, I'll be there cleaning out the kennels or walking them, and it's fine, but I don't get that spark from it like I used to. Speaking of the kennels, they just switched the brand of cleaner they use, and man, it smells like straight industrial bleach mixed with cheap pine needles. It gives me a headache if I'm in the back room for too long. I told the manager, Sarah—she's a nice lady, used to work at the post office before she retired—I told her we need to go back to the old stuff. She said it's a budget thing, which I get, you know? Everything's getting more expensive. Even diesel for my truck is up another ten cents this week. But yeah, the shelter... I just kind of show up and do the work, and then I go home.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel pleasure or happiness at all anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? Place is supposed to have the best meatloaf. I used to really look forward to a good meatloaf, you know, with the ketchup glaze baked on top like my mom used to make. But this place, they put some kind of weird brown gravy all over it. Swear it looked just like 10W-30 motor oil. And the waitress, she was nice enough, but she kept calling me \"hon\" in this real high-pitched voice. Sounded exactly like my ex-wife's sister, Brenda. Man, Brenda had this little toy poodle that just barked at the wind. I swear that dog didn't sleep, ever. We had to watch it for a week back in '08 and it chewed up all the wooden baseboards in our front hallway. Cost me a couple hundred bucks from my security deposit when we finally moved out of that rental... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't know. I just don't really feel much of anything most of the time. Just flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. We had his service out at this little cemetery near the county line, and I remember it was raining so hard the mud was up to our ankles. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. You know, speaking of routines, they just changed up my route at work. Used to be I'd take the 95 straight down, hit the same three stops by noon. Now they've got me cutting through these back roads, and let me tell you, the potholes on County Road 9 are something else. I hit one last Tuesday that practically rattled my teeth loose. Spilled half my coffee right onto the dashboard. And it wasn't even the good coffee, it was that cheap stuff from the breakroom that tastes like burnt popcorn. \n\nI tried telling dispatch about the roads, this guy named Gary. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, \"Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road.\" He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. It's just been the same heavy feeling the whole time.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any things that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really... no. I can't really think of anything that brings me any kind of joy right now. I used to like grabbing a hot meal at the end of a long haul, but lately food is just food, you know? There used to be this diner I'd always hit up right off I-80. Marge's Diner. She made this meatloaf that was just out of this world. Smothered in onions and brown gravy. 

But she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. I mean, who puts avocado on a turkey club? It just slides right out the sides when you take a bite. Makes a huge mess on the steering wheel if you are trying to eat while you drive. I had to use half a roll of paper towels to get the grease off my hands last time I stopped there. And those paper towels they sell at the Pilot stations now, they are completely useless. One wipe and they just disintegrate. It is like trying to clean up with cheap tissue paper. You end up with little white fuzzies all over your hands and the gearshift. 

But, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no. Nothing really gives me that spark anymore. It is all just kind of gray.

It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? Place is supposed to have the best meatloaf. I used to really look forward to a good meatloaf, you know, with the ketchup glaze baked on top like my mom used to make. But this place, they put some kind of weird brown gravy all over it. Swear it looked just like 10W-30 motor oil. And the waitress, she was nice enough, but she kept calling me \"hon\" in this real high-pitched voice. Sounded exactly like my ex-wife's sister, Brenda. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. You know, speaking of routines, they just changed up my route at work. Used to be I'd take the 95 straight down, hit the same three stops by noon. Now they've got me cutting through these back roads, and let me tell you, the potholes on County Road 9 are something else. I hit one last Tuesday that practically rattled my teeth loose. Spilled half my coffee right onto the dashboard. And it wasn't even the good coffee, it was that cheap stuff from the breakroom that tastes like burnt popcorn. \n\nI tried telling dispatch about the roads, this guy named Gary. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, \"Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road.\" He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. There was this place off exit 42, hand-wash only. The guy who ran it, Sal, he had this old pressure washer that sounded like a jet engine taking off. You could hear it from the highway. Sal was always trying to sell me on these \"premium wax\" packages. Said it would make the rig shine like a new penny. I finally bought it once, back in... maybe 2018? Cost me forty bucks extra. And wouldn't you know it, it rained the very next morning. Just my luck, right? Rained so hard the wipers couldn't keep up. That's the thing about weather around here, it just turns on a dime. Like last week, they said it was going to be sunny, and I ended up driving through a hail storm near Scranton. Sounded like gravel hitting the roof. \n\nBut, uh... no. No periods where I felt better. It's just been the same heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really... no. I can't really think of anything that brings me any kind of joy right now. I used to like grabbing a hot meal at the end of a long haul, but lately food is just food, you know? There used to be this diner I'd always hit up right off I-80. Marge's Diner. She made this meatloaf that was just out of this world. Smothered in onions and brown gravy. \n\nBut she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. I mean, who puts avocado on a turkey club? It just slides right out the sides when you take a bite. Makes a huge mess on the steering wheel if you are trying to eat while you drive. I had to use half a roll of paper towels to get the grease off my hands last time I stopped there. And those paper towels they sell at the Pilot stations now, they are completely useless. One wipe and they just disintegrate. It is like trying to clean up with cheap tissue paper. You end up with little white fuzzies all over your hands and the gearshift. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no. Nothing really gives me that spark anymore. It is all just kind of gray.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess so. I mean, like I said, I don't really care about much anymore. Even the stuff I used to like... it's just a chore now. Take fishing, right? I used to love taking the boat out on Lake Hopatcong. Get out there before the sun comes up, just you and the water. It was quiet. 

But then they started building all those new condos on the east shore. Just massive, ugly things blocking the view. And the traffic on Route 15 getting up there on a Saturday? Forget about it. It's bumper to bumper all the way from Jefferson. Last time I went, I sat behind this guy hauling a camper who kept riding his brakes the whole way up the mountain. I could smell burning brake pads for three miles. And you know how much a brake job costs on a rig these days? My mechanic, Dave, he quoted me twelve hundred just for the front axle. Twelve hundred! And Dave's supposed to be giving me the buddy discount because we went to high school together. We were in shop class with Mr. Henderson. Henderson was missing half his index finger from a bandsaw accident... 

Anyway, I haven't taken the boat out in... I don't even know. Months. I just don't have the energy or the interest to deal with it all anymore. It's easier to just stay home.

Smothered in onions and brown gravy. \n\nBut she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. It is all just kind of gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, like I said, I don't really care about much anymore. Even the stuff I used to like... it's just a chore now. Take fishing, right? I used to love taking the boat out on Lake Hopatcong. Get out there before the sun comes up, just you and the water. It was quiet. \n\nBut then they started building all those new condos on the east shore. Just massive, ugly things blocking the view. And the traffic on Route 15 getting up there on a Saturday? Forget about it. It's bumper to bumper all the way from Jefferson. Last time I went, I sat behind this guy hauling a camper who kept riding his brakes the whole way up the mountain. I could smell burning brake pads for three miles. And you know how much a brake job costs on a rig these days? My mechanic, Dave, he quoted me twelve hundred just for the front axle. Twelve hundred! And Dave's supposed to be giving me the buddy discount because we went to high school together. We were in shop class with Mr. Henderson. Henderson was missing half his index finger from a bandsaw accident... \n\nAnyway, I haven't taken the boat out in... I don't even know. Months. I just don't have the energy or the interest to deal with it all anymore. It's easier to just stay home.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My appetite is about the same, I guess. But sleep... sleep's a different story. On nights before a run, I'll just lie there in the dark, thinking about all the stops I have to make the next day. Or I'll wake up around four in the morning and that's it, I can't really get back to sleep. My alarm clock has those big red numbers, the kind that are way too bright. I bought it at this hardware store in town, Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller used to run it, but his son took over a few years ago. The son, he doesn't know a Phillips head from a flathead, I swear. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. And speaking of wind, the crosswinds on the George Washington Bridge have been brutal lately. If you're running empty, you feel every single gust. You have to grip the wheel so tight your knuckles turn white. 

But anyway, yeah, because of the sleep thing, even when I make it to work on time, I feel like I'm starting the day already worn down. Just exhausted before I even turn the key.

We had to watch it for a week back in '08 and it chewed up all the wooden baseboards in our front hallway. Cost me a couple hundred bucks from my security deposit when we finally moved out of that rental... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't know. I just don't really feel much of anything most of the time. Just flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. We had his service out at this little cemetery near the county line, and I remember it was raining so hard the mud was up to our ankles. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. You know, speaking of routines, they just changed up my route at work. Used to be I'd take the 95 straight down, hit the same three stops by noon. Now they've got me cutting through these back roads, and let me tell you, the potholes on County Road 9 are something else. I hit one last Tuesday that practically rattled my teeth loose. Spilled half my coffee right onto the dashboard. And it wasn't even the good coffee, it was that cheap stuff from the breakroom that tastes like burnt popcorn. \n\nI tried telling dispatch about the roads, this guy named Gary. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, \"Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road.\" He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. There was this place off exit 42, hand-wash only. The guy who ran it, Sal, he had this old pressure washer that sounded like a jet engine taking off. You could hear it from the highway. Sal was always trying to sell me on these \"premium wax\" packages. Said it would make the rig shine like a new penny. I finally bought it once, back in... maybe 2018? Cost me forty bucks extra. And wouldn't you know it, it rained the very next morning. Just my luck, right? Rained so hard the wipers couldn't keep up. That's the thing about weather around here, it just turns on a dime. Like last week, they said it was going to be sunny, and I ended up driving through a hail storm near Scranton. Sounded like gravel hitting the roof. \n\nBut, uh... no. No periods where I felt better. It's just been the same heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really... no. I can't really think of anything that brings me any kind of joy right now. I used to like grabbing a hot meal at the end of a long haul, but lately food is just food, you know? There used to be this diner I'd always hit up right off I-80. Marge's Diner. She made this meatloaf that was just out of this world. Smothered in onions and brown gravy. \n\nBut she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. I mean, who puts avocado on a turkey club? It just slides right out the sides when you take a bite. Makes a huge mess on the steering wheel if you are trying to eat while you drive. I had to use half a roll of paper towels to get the grease off my hands last time I stopped there. And those paper towels they sell at the Pilot stations now, they are completely useless. One wipe and they just disintegrate. It is like trying to clean up with cheap tissue paper. You end up with little white fuzzies all over your hands and the gearshift. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no. Nothing really gives me that spark anymore. It is all just kind of gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, like I said, I don't really care about much anymore. Even the stuff I used to like... it's just a chore now. Take fishing, right? I used to love taking the boat out on Lake Hopatcong. Get out there before the sun comes up, just you and the water. It was quiet. \n\nBut then they started building all those new condos on the east shore. Just massive, ugly things blocking the view. And the traffic on Route 15 getting up there on a Saturday? Forget about it. It's bumper to bumper all the way from Jefferson. Last time I went, I sat behind this guy hauling a camper who kept riding his brakes the whole way up the mountain. I could smell burning brake pads for three miles. And you know how much a brake job costs on a rig these days? My mechanic, Dave, he quoted me twelve hundred just for the front axle. Twelve hundred! And Dave's supposed to be giving me the buddy discount because we went to high school together. We were in shop class with Mr. Henderson. Henderson was missing half his index finger from a bandsaw accident... \n\nAnyway, I haven't taken the boat out in... I don't even know. Months. I just don't have the energy or the interest to deal with it all anymore. It's easier to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: My appetite is about the same, I guess. But sleep... sleep's a different story. On nights before a run, I'll just lie there in the dark, thinking about all the stops I have to make the next day. Or I'll wake up around four in the morning and that's it, I can't really get back to sleep. My alarm clock has those big red numbers, the kind that are way too bright. I bought it at this hardware store in town, Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller used to run it, but his son took over a few years ago. The son, he doesn't know a Phillips head from a flathead, I swear. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. And speaking of wind, the crosswinds on the George Washington Bridge have been brutal lately. If you're running empty, you feel every single gust. You have to grip the wheel so tight your knuckles turn white. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, because of the sleep thing, even when I make it to work on time, I feel like I'm starting the day already worn down. Just exhausted before I even turn the key.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Look forward to? No, not really. I mean, I can't think of a single thing. It's just the exact same routine every single day. 

I used to kinda look forward to my morning coffee, getting it from this little drive-thru shack on Route 9. The lady working there always knew I wanted it black with two sugars. But they tore it down a few months ago to build another one of those massive chain pharmacies. Like we really need another pharmacy. There's already one on every single corner. And the parking lots for those places are always designed terribly if you're driving anything bigger than a sedan. They put those concrete parking blocks right where you need to swing wide. I caught my rear tire on one last month and it took a chunk right out of the sidewall. 

Do you know how hard it is to get a decent tire replaced when you're out on the road? You call dispatch and they put you on hold for twenty minutes, listening to that awful elevator music that sounds like it's being played underwater. My dispatcher, Gary, he's completely useless anyway. He's always too busy managing his fantasy football team to actually do his job. Last week he routed me right through downtown Scranton during some kind of street fair. I'm sitting there in a 53-foot trailer trying not to run over a funnel cake stand. 

But, uh, yeah. No. To answer your question, there's nothing I really look forward to. The days just kind of blur together into one long drive.

They've had this one lane closed for construction for what feels like three years, and it just backs everything up for miles. We actually just got a litter of beagle mixes in that chew on absolutely everything. One of them got ahold of my good work boots last Tuesday and just went to town on the laces. But yeah, even with all that going on, it just feels like I'm going through the motions. Like I'm not really present in my own life, you know? Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy in the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering at the animal shelter?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if \"joy\" is the right word anymore. Speaking of the kennels, they just switched the brand of cleaner they use, and man, it smells like straight industrial bleach mixed with cheap pine needles. It gives me a headache if I'm in the back room for too long. I told the manager, Sarah—she's a nice lady, used to work at the post office before she retired—I told her we need to go back to the old stuff. She said it's a budget thing, which I get, you know? Everything's getting more expensive. Even diesel for my truck is up another ten cents this week. But yeah, the shelter... I just kind of show up and do the work, and then I go home.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel pleasure or happiness at all anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? Place is supposed to have the best meatloaf. I used to really look forward to a good meatloaf, you know, with the ketchup glaze baked on top like my mom used to make. But this place, they put some kind of weird brown gravy all over it. Swear it looked just like 10W-30 motor oil. And the waitress, she was nice enough, but she kept calling me \"hon\" in this real high-pitched voice. Sounded exactly like my ex-wife's sister, Brenda. I just don't really feel much of anything most of the time. Just flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. We had his service out at this little cemetery near the county line, and I remember it was raining so hard the mud was up to our ankles. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, \"Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road.\" He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. There was this place off exit 42, hand-wash only. The guy who ran it, Sal, he had this old pressure washer that sounded like a jet engine taking off. You could hear it from the highway. Sal was always trying to sell me on these \"premium wax\" packages. Said it would make the rig shine like a new penny. I finally bought it once, back in... maybe 2018? Cost me forty bucks extra. And wouldn't you know it, it rained the very next morning. Just my luck, right? Rained so hard the wipers couldn't keep up. That's the thing about weather around here, it just turns on a dime. Like last week, they said it was going to be sunny, and I ended up driving through a hail storm near Scranton. Sounded like gravel hitting the roof. \n\nBut, uh... no. No periods where I felt better. It's just been the same heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really... no. I can't really think of anything that brings me any kind of joy right now. I used to like grabbing a hot meal at the end of a long haul, but lately food is just food, you know? There used to be this diner I'd always hit up right off I-80. Marge's Diner. She made this meatloaf that was just out of this world. Smothered in onions and brown gravy. \n\nBut she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. I mean, who puts avocado on a turkey club? It just slides right out the sides when you take a bite. Makes a huge mess on the steering wheel if you are trying to eat while you drive. I had to use half a roll of paper towels to get the grease off my hands last time I stopped there. And those paper towels they sell at the Pilot stations now, they are completely useless. One wipe and they just disintegrate. It is like trying to clean up with cheap tissue paper. You end up with little white fuzzies all over your hands and the gearshift. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no. Nothing really gives me that spark anymore. It is all just kind of gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, like I said, I don't really care about much anymore. Even the stuff I used to like... it's just a chore now. Take fishing, right? I used to love taking the boat out on Lake Hopatcong. Get out there before the sun comes up, just you and the water. It was quiet. \n\nBut then they started building all those new condos on the east shore. Just massive, ugly things blocking the view. And the traffic on Route 15 getting up there on a Saturday? Forget about it. It's bumper to bumper all the way from Jefferson. Last time I went, I sat behind this guy hauling a camper who kept riding his brakes the whole way up the mountain. I could smell burning brake pads for three miles. And you know how much a brake job costs on a rig these days? My mechanic, Dave, he quoted me twelve hundred just for the front axle. Twelve hundred! And Dave's supposed to be giving me the buddy discount because we went to high school together. We were in shop class with Mr. Henderson. Henderson was missing half his index finger from a bandsaw accident... \n\nAnyway, I haven't taken the boat out in... I don't even know. Months. I just don't have the energy or the interest to deal with it all anymore. It's easier to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: My appetite is about the same, I guess. But sleep... sleep's a different story. On nights before a run, I'll just lie there in the dark, thinking about all the stops I have to make the next day. Or I'll wake up around four in the morning and that's it, I can't really get back to sleep. My alarm clock has those big red numbers, the kind that are way too bright. I bought it at this hardware store in town, Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller used to run it, but his son took over a few years ago. The son, he doesn't know a Phillips head from a flathead, I swear. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. And speaking of wind, the crosswinds on the George Washington Bridge have been brutal lately. If you're running empty, you feel every single gust. You have to grip the wheel so tight your knuckles turn white. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, because of the sleep thing, even when I make it to work on time, I feel like I'm starting the day already worn down. Just exhausted before I even turn the key.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that happen during your day that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No, not really. I mean, I can't think of a single thing. It's just the exact same routine every single day. \n\nI used to kinda look forward to my morning coffee, getting it from this little drive-thru shack on Route 9. The lady working there always knew I wanted it black with two sugars. But they tore it down a few months ago to build another one of those massive chain pharmacies. Like we really need another pharmacy. There's already one on every single corner. And the parking lots for those places are always designed terribly if you're driving anything bigger than a sedan. They put those concrete parking blocks right where you need to swing wide. I caught my rear tire on one last month and it took a chunk right out of the sidewall. \n\nDo you know how hard it is to get a decent tire replaced when you're out on the road? You call dispatch and they put you on hold for twenty minutes, listening to that awful elevator music that sounds like it's being played underwater. My dispatcher, Gary, he's completely useless anyway. He's always too busy managing his fantasy football team to actually do his job. Last week he routed me right through downtown Scranton during some kind of street fair. I'm sitting there in a 53-foot trailer trying not to run over a funnel cake stand. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. No. To answer your question, there's nothing I really look forward to. The days just kind of blur together into one long drive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Or I'll wake up around four in the morning and that's it, I can't really get back to sleep. My alarm clock has those big red numbers, the kind that are way too bright. I bought it at this hardware store in town, Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller used to run it, but his son took over a few years ago. The son, he doesn't know a Phillips head from a flathead, I swear. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. And speaking of wind, the crosswinds on the George Washington Bridge have been brutal lately. If you're running empty, you feel every single gust. You have to grip the wheel so tight your knuckles turn white. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, because of the sleep thing, even when I make it to work on time, I feel like I'm starting the day already worn down. Just exhausted before I even turn the key.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that happen during your day that you look forward to?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No, not really. I mean, I can't think of a single thing. It's just the exact same routine every single day. \n\nI used to kinda look forward to my morning coffee, getting it from this little drive-thru shack on Route 9. The lady working there always knew I wanted it black with two sugars. But they tore it down a few months ago to build another one of those massive chain pharmacies. Like we really need another pharmacy. There's already one on every single corner. And the parking lots for those places are always designed terribly if you're driving anything bigger than a sedan. They put those concrete parking blocks right where you need to swing wide. I caught my rear tire on one last month and it took a chunk right out of the sidewall. \n\nDo you know how hard it is to get a decent tire replaced when you're out on the road? You call dispatch and they put you on hold for twenty minutes, listening to that awful elevator music that sounds like it's being played underwater. My dispatcher, Gary, he's completely useless anyway. He's always too busy managing his fantasy football team to actually do his job. Last week he routed me right through downtown Scranton during some kind of street fair. I'm sitting there in a 53-foot trailer trying not to run over a funnel cake stand. \n\nBut, uh, yeah. No. To answer your question, there's nothing I really look forward to. The days just kind of blur together into one long drive.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... didn't you just ask me that? About losing interest in stuff? I thought I just said that about the fishing. Like I said, it's all just a chore now. I don't really enjoy much of anything most days. It's just... flat. 

Like, even when I'm off the road and I could be doing something, I just sit there. The television's on, but I'm not really watching it. They have these shows on now, these reality shows about people doing the exact same job I do. Ice Road Truckers, stuff like that. Who watches that? I do it for a living, why would I want to watch someone else do it on TV? And half the time, the stuff they show isn't even realistic. They make a big deal out of sliding on a little patch of ice. Try hitting black ice on I-80 in Pennsylvania with a full load of steel coils behind you. That'll wake you up faster than a pot of truck stop coffee. I knew a guy, Tommy... he hauled steel out of Pittsburgh. He hit a patch of ice near Clarion and those coils shifted. Snapped the chains right off. They had to bring out two heavy wreckers just to clear the highway. They shut down the eastbound lanes for eight hours. Eight hours! Can you imagine being stuck in that backup? You can't even get out to stretch your legs because it's freezing, and you're just burning diesel to stay warm... 

But yeah, like I said before. I don't really enjoy things anymore. It's just easier not to bother.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thanks for seeing me. I came in because... I don't know, for a long time now there's just been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me most days. I'm still getting through my driving routes, though. I drive a truck, mostly regional, and let me tell you, the traffic on the interstate lately has been an absolute joke. They've had this one lane closed for construction for what feels like three years, and it just backs everything up for miles. I sit there burning daylight just staring at the bumper in front of me. \n\nAnyway, I still do that, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts at the animal shelter. We actually just got a litter of beagle mixes in that chew on absolutely everything. One of them got ahold of my good work boots last Tuesday and just went to town on the laces. But yeah, even with all that going on, it just feels like I'm going through the motions. Like I'm not really present in my own life, you know? Just... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find joy in the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering at the animal shelter?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if \"joy\" is the right word anymore. I still go, but... it's just something I do. Like, I'll be there cleaning out the kennels or walking them, and it's fine, but I don't get that spark from it like I used to. Speaking of the kennels, they just switched the brand of cleaner they use, and man, it smells like straight industrial bleach mixed with cheap pine needles. It gives me a headache if I'm in the back room for too long. I told the manager, Sarah—she's a nice lady, used to work at the post office before she retired—I told her we need to go back to the old stuff. She said it's a budget thing, which I get, you know? Everything's getting more expensive. Even diesel for my truck is up another ten cents this week. But yeah, the shelter... I just kind of show up and do the work, and then I go home.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel pleasure or happiness at all anymore?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. It's hard to say what happiness even feels like these days. I guess maybe not really? Like, I stopped at this diner off Route 81 the other day, right? Place is supposed to have the best meatloaf. I used to really look forward to a good meatloaf, you know, with the ketchup glaze baked on top like my mom used to make. But this place, they put some kind of weird brown gravy all over it. Swear it looked just like 10W-30 motor oil. And the waitress, she was nice enough, but she kept calling me \"hon\" in this real high-pitched voice. Sounded exactly like my ex-wife's sister, Brenda. Man, Brenda had this little toy poodle that just barked at the wind. I swear that dog didn't sleep, ever. We had to watch it for a week back in '08 and it chewed up all the wooden baseboards in our front hallway. Cost me a couple hundred bucks from my security deposit when we finally moved out of that rental... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't know. I just don't really feel much of anything most of the time. Just flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a long time. I'd say about three years now. Yeah, right around the time my dad passed away. That was a tough time. We had his service out at this little cemetery near the county line, and I remember it was raining so hard the mud was up to our ankles. Ruined a perfectly good pair of dress shoes I bought just for the occasion. They were those slip-ons, you know, with the little tassels? I thought they looked sharp, but they just soaked right through. \n\nI tried to clean them up afterwards with this leather polish I got from a guy at the truck stop, but they never looked the same. That guy at the truck stop, though, he was a real character. Sold everything out of the back of his rig—CB radios, bootleg DVDs, you name it. He tried to sell me this oversized steering wheel cover that looked like leopard print. I told him, \"Buddy, I'm driving a Peterbilt, not a disco ball.\" But anyway, yeah, it's been about three years since my dad died, and that heavy feeling just kind of set in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean... not really. I don't really look forward to much of anything these days. It's just kind of wake up, get through the day, go to sleep. Same old routine. You know, speaking of routines, they just changed up my route at work. Used to be I'd take the 95 straight down, hit the same three stops by noon. Now they've got me cutting through these back roads, and let me tell you, the potholes on County Road 9 are something else. I hit one last Tuesday that practically rattled my teeth loose. Spilled half my coffee right onto the dashboard. And it wasn't even the good coffee, it was that cheap stuff from the breakroom that tastes like burnt popcorn. \n\nI tried telling dispatch about the roads, this guy named Gary. Gary's always eating those little powdered donuts, you know the ones? Gets the white dust all over his keyboard. I told him, \"Gary, you're gonna break an axle sending guys down that road.\" He just sort of shrugged and took another bite. Honestly, I don't think he even listens half the time. But yeah, as far as looking forward to things... I just don't. It's all just kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of even a few days where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. No, not really. It's just been this same low feeling, day in, day out. Never really lifts, you know? Not even for a weekend. Weekends used to be when I'd get the truck washed. There was this place off exit 42, hand-wash only. The guy who ran it, Sal, he had this old pressure washer that sounded like a jet engine taking off. You could hear it from the highway. Sal was always trying to sell me on these \"premium wax\" packages. Said it would make the rig shine like a new penny. I finally bought it once, back in... maybe 2018? Cost me forty bucks extra. And wouldn't you know it, it rained the very next morning. Just my luck, right? Rained so hard the wipers couldn't keep up. That's the thing about weather around here, it just turns on a dime. Like last week, they said it was going to be sunny, and I ended up driving through a hail storm near Scranton. Sounded like gravel hitting the roof. \n\nBut, uh... no. No periods where I felt better. It's just been the same heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any things that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really... no. I can't really think of anything that brings me any kind of joy right now. I used to like grabbing a hot meal at the end of a long haul, but lately food is just food, you know? There used to be this diner I'd always hit up right off I-80. Marge's Diner. She made this meatloaf that was just out of this world. Smothered in onions and brown gravy. \n\nBut she sold the place a couple years back to this guy from Chicago, and he came in and completely changed the menu. Started putting avocado on everything. I mean, who puts avocado on a turkey club? It just slides right out the sides when you take a bite. Makes a huge mess on the steering wheel if you are trying to eat while you drive. I had to use half a roll of paper towels to get the grease off my hands last time I stopped there. And those paper towels they sell at the Pilot stations now, they are completely useless. One wipe and they just disintegrate. It is like trying to clean up with cheap tissue paper. You end up with little white fuzzies all over your hands and the gearshift. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no. Nothing really gives me that spark anymore. It is all just kind of gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, like I said, I don't really care about much anymore. Even the stuff I used to like... it's just a chore now. Take fishing, right? I used to love taking the boat out on Lake Hopatcong. Get out there before the sun comes up, just you and the water. It was quiet. \n\nBut then they started building all those new condos on the east shore. Just massive, ugly things blocking the view. And the traffic on Route 15 getting up there on a Saturday? Forget about it. It's bumper to bumper all the way from Jefferson. Last time I went, I sat behind this guy hauling a camper who kept riding his brakes the whole way up the mountain. I could smell burning brake pads for three miles. And you know how much a brake job costs on a rig these days? My mechanic, Dave, he quoted me twelve hundred just for the front axle. Twelve hundred! And Dave's supposed to be giving me the buddy discount because we went to high school together. We were in shop class with Mr. Henderson. Henderson was missing half his index finger from a bandsaw accident... \n\nAnyway, I haven't taken the boat out in... I don't even know. Months. I just don't have the energy or the interest to deal with it all anymore. It's easier to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: My appetite is about the same, I guess. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. It's easier to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: My appetite is about the same, I guess. I bought it at this hardware store in town, Miller's Hardware. Old man Miller used to run it, but his son took over a few years ago. The son, he doesn't know a Phillips head from a flathead, I swear. I went in there looking for a specific kind of sealant for the truck's weather stripping, and he tried to sell me bathroom caulk. Bathroom caulk! Can you imagine that holding up on the interstate at 70 miles an hour? The wind would just rip it right off. And speaking of wind, the crosswinds on the George Washington Bridge have been brutal lately. If you're running empty, you feel every single gust. Well, I mean, I'm still showing up for work, if that's what you mean. I still get the routes done. I still go down to the food pantry on the weekends like I usually do, but... man, everything just takes so much more pushing than it used to. It's like dragging a heavy tarp over a load of lumber in the rain. 

Speaking of lumber, the yard I pick up from over in Jersey, they changed their whole loading procedure last month. Used to be you'd just pull up to the bay, hand your slip to the forklift guy—this guy named Sal, great guy, always had a joke—and he'd load you up in ten minutes flat. Now they've got this new automated system where you have to scan a barcode on your phone before you even get past the gate. Half the time the scanner doesn't work if there's a glare from the sun, and you're just sitting there blocking traffic while some kid in a neon vest tries to manually type in a twenty-digit number. And don't get me started on the forklift guys they have now. Sal retired, moved down to Florida. Bought a little place in Sarasota, I think. Or maybe it was Naples. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, the new guys, they drop the pallets so hard you can feel the whole cab shake. I had to get out and yell at one of them last week because he almost took my mirror off. Mirrors on these rigs aren't cheap, you know. You crack one of those convex mirrors and dispatch acts like you just asked them to pay for a new engine. 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, work. Yeah, I'm still doing it. But I actually started filling out an application for a logistics supervisor position a while back. Get me off the road, you know? Put me behind a desk. But I just left it sitting there unfinished. I look at it and I just... I don't trust myself to keep up with it right now. It just feels like too much.
